## Architectural Review: Desloppify

### Overview

Desloppify is a ~4,500-line Python CLI tool for detecting and tracking technical debt across codebases. It has a plugin system for language support (Python and TypeScript), a persistent state store, a dimension-based scoring system, and an LLM-oriented narrative layer. The architecture is broadly sound for a single-developer CLI tool, but there are several systemic issues that would become problems at scale or if the tool were used as a library.

---

### 1. Circular Dependencies

**Severity: Medium**

There is an implicit circular dependency between `cli.py` and the command modules in `commands/`. The commands import private functions from `cli.py`:

```python
# desloppify/commands/scan.py:6
from ..cli import _state_path, _write_query

# desloppify/commands/scan.py:175
from ..cli import _resolve_lang
```

Every command module (`scan.py`, `status.py`, `show.py`, `next.py`, `resolve.py`, `fix_cmd.py`, `detect.py`, `zone_cmd.py`, and even `visualize.py`) imports `_state_path`, `_write_query`, or `_resolve_lang` from `cli.py`. Meanwhile, `cli.py` imports all those command modules:

```python
# desloppify/cli.py:246-253
from .commands.scan import cmd_scan
from .commands.status import cmd_status
# ... etc.
```

This works at runtime because `cli.py` uses deferred imports inside `main()`, but it is a structural circular dependency. The private functions `_state_path`, `_write_query`, and `_resolve_lang` are really shared utilities that should live in their own module (e.g., a `desloppify/shared.py` or move them into `utils.py`). Their leading underscores signal "private to this module," but they are in fact the most-imported functions in the entire codebase.

**No true circular import between `plan.py` and `narrative.py`** -- they import from `scoring.py` independently. The import graph is otherwise clean: detectors depend on `utils`, `state`, and `detectors/base`; language plugins depend on the base layer and shared detectors. There are no cycles among the non-CLI modules.

---

### 2. Inconsistent Abstractions

**Severity: Low-Medium**

The `LangConfig` dataclass in `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/base.py` uses `Callable` types extensively without protocols or type signatures:

```python
# desloppify/lang/base.py:56
build_dep_graph: Callable[[Path], dict]
# ...
file_finder: Callable | None = None          # no signature at all
extract_functions: Callable[[str], list[dict]] | None = None
```

The return types are all `dict` or `list[dict]` -- there are no typed protocols for what a "graph" dict looks like, what a "finding" dict contains, or what structure detector results must follow. The entire data layer is untyped dicts. This means:
- A detector returning a dict missing a required key silently propagates until `make_finding()` or `merge_scan()` blows up.
- The `DetectorPhase.run` signature is `Callable[[Path, LangConfig], list[dict]]` but some phases return `tuple[list[dict], dict[str, int]]` (findings + potentials). The caller in `_generate_findings_from_lang()` handles this with an `isinstance` check:

```python
# desloppify/plan.py:73-77
if isinstance(result, tuple):
    phase_findings, phase_potentials = result
else:
    phase_findings = result  # backward compat
```

This "backward compat" branch means the type system cannot enforce that phases return potentials. Any new phase that forgets the tuple will silently have zero potentials, which degrades scoring accuracy.

The `_zone_map` field on `LangConfig` is typed as `object` and mutated externally at scan time by `plan.py`. This is a convention, not an enforced contract.

---

### 3. Global Mutable State

**Severity: High -- the biggest systemic issue**

There are three pieces of global mutable state that would be problematic for library use, concurrent execution, or testing:

**a) `_extra_exclusions` in `utils.py`** (line 24):
```python
_extra_exclusions: tuple[str, ...] = ()
```
Set once by `set_exclusions()` from CLI startup, then accessed by `_find_source_files_cached()`, `finalize_graph()`, and the Python unused detector. The MEMORY.md file itself documents the binding hazard:
> Other modules MUST import the module and access `_utils_mod._extra_exclusions` (not `from utils import _extra_exclusions`) to see runtime updates.

The graph module (`/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/detectors/graph.py:18-19`) correctly uses the module-level access pattern:
```python
from .. import utils as _utils_mod
_extra_exclusions = _utils_mod._extra_exclusions
```
But `scan.py` directly imports the name:
```python
# commands/scan.py:188
from ..utils import rel, _extra_exclusions, PROJECT_ROOT
```
This works today only because `set_exclusions()` is called before the scan command runs, but it is fragile. A future refactor that changes import timing would silently break exclusion behavior.

**b) `PROJECT_ROOT` in `utils.py`** (line 10):
```python
PROJECT_ROOT = Path(os.environ.get("DESLOPPIFY_ROOT", Path.cwd())).resolve()
```
This is computed once at import time from `os.getcwd()`. It is imported by nearly every module. If `os.chdir()` happens after import, or if the tool is used as a library from a different working directory, `PROJECT_ROOT` will be wrong. This is a classic Python gotcha.

**c) `_registry` in `lang/__init__.py`** (line 7):
```python
_registry: dict[str, type] = {}
```
Module-level mutable dict populated by the `@register_lang` decorator. Benign for CLI use but prevents testing language plugins in isolation.

**d) `lang._zone_map` mutation** -- The `LangConfig` instance has `_zone_map` set to `None` at construction and then mutated by `plan.py:57`:
```python
lang._zone_map = FileZoneMap(files, lang.zone_rules, ...)
```
Every phase runner then reads `lang._zone_map`. This is implicit shared state threaded through a mutable object, rather than passed explicitly as a parameter.

**e) `lru_cache` on `_find_source_files_cached`** -- The cache is keyed on `(path, extensions, exclusions)` but `_extra_exclusions` is accessed as a global inside the function, not as a cache key parameter. The `set_exclusions()` function does call `_find_source_files_cached.cache_clear()`, but this is an easy thing to forget if the function is modified.

---

### 4. Import Hygiene

**Severity: Low**

No star imports in production code. The codebase correctly uses explicit imports everywhere.

However, many modules import private names (prefixed with `_`) from other modules:
- `_state_path`, `_write_query`, `_resolve_lang` from `cli.py` (used by every command)
- `_extra_exclusions` from `utils.py`
- `_preloaded_state` stuffed onto the `args` namespace object

Import ordering is inconsistent -- some files do standard library first, then local; others mix them. This is cosmetic.

Deferred imports inside functions are used extensively, particularly in `cli.py` and the command modules. This is appropriate for avoiding circular imports and speeding up startup, but makes the dependency graph harder to trace statically.

---

### 5. Error Propagation

**Severity: Medium**

Error handling follows a consistent pattern: file I/O operations catch `(OSError, UnicodeDecodeError)` and silently skip the file. This is the right behavior for a scanner that should be resilient to unreadable files.

However, there are several concerns:

**a) Swallowed ImportErrors in `lang/__init__.py`**:
```python
# lang/__init__.py:110-118
try:
    importlib.import_module(f".{module_name}", __package__)
except ImportError:
    pass
```
If a language plugin has a broken import (e.g., a missing dependency), it is silently suppressed. The user sees no error -- the language simply does not appear in the registry. A malformed `__init__.py` in a language plugin would be invisible.

**b) Swallowed scorecard ImportError in `scan.py`**:
```python
# commands/scan.py:288
except ImportError:
    pass  # Pillow not installed — skip silently
```
This is intentional (Pillow is optional), but the bare `ImportError` could mask actual import failures within the scorecard module.

**c) Double state loading** -- The `cmd_scan` function loads state twice:
```python
# cli.py:240 (in main)
state = load_state(sp)
args._preloaded_state = state

# commands/scan.py:161,165
state = load_state(sp)
```
The scan command ignores `args._preloaded_state` and loads state fresh. This is actually correct behavior (the scan needs the freshest state), but the preloaded state in `cli.py:main()` was loaded specifically for `_apply_persisted_exclusions()`. The dual-load is wasteful and confusing. Other commands also re-load state independently (e.g., `resolve.py:18`, `fix_cmd.py:83`), ignoring the preloaded copy on `args`.

**d) `sys.exit(1)` in utility functions** -- `_resolve_lang` in `cli.py` calls `sys.exit(1)` on error. This prevents the tool from being used as a library where you would want to catch exceptions instead.

---

### 6. Data Flow

**Severity: Medium-High**

The primary data flow for a scan is:

1. `cli.py:main()` parses args, resolves lang, loads state, applies exclusions to a global
2. `cmd_scan(args)` loads state again, calls `generate_findings(path, lang=lang)`
3. `generate_findings` sets `lang._zone_map` (side effect on lang object), runs phases
4. Each phase accesses `lang._zone_map` and `_extra_exclusions` (globals)
5. Phases return `(findings, potentials)` or just `findings`
6. `merge_scan()` merges findings into state dict (mutation), returns diff
7. `save_state()` persists to disk
8. `_write_query()` writes LLM-readable JSON to `.desloppify/query.json`

The state dict is passed around and mutated in place. There is no immutability guarantee. The `merge_scan()` function modifies the state dict's `findings`, `potentials`, `scan_history`, and `stats` directly. This is efficient but fragile -- if any caller holds a reference to the old findings dict, they will see post-merge data.

The narrative system (`narrative.py`) also mutates state by updating `reminder_history`:
```python
# narrative.py:785-786
state["reminder_history"] = reminder_history
```
This side effect inside a function named `compute_narrative` (which sounds like a pure computation) is surprising.

The `args` namespace is used as a grab-bag for passing state between the CLI layer and commands: `args._preloaded_state`, `args._state_path`, `args.path`. This conflates parsed CLI arguments with runtime state.

---

### 7. Testing Gaps

**Severity: High**

There are **zero test files** in the repository. No `tests/` directory, no `test_*.py` files, no `conftest.py`. The MEMORY.md mentions testing commands that run the tool on itself:
```
python -m desloppify --lang python scan --path desloppify
```

This is integration testing via manual CLI invocation, not automated testing. The codebase has several properties that make it hard to test:

- Global mutable state (`PROJECT_ROOT`, `_extra_exclusions`) must be reset between tests
- `sys.exit(1)` calls in utility functions prevent unit testing
- All output goes to `print()` / `sys.stderr` rather than returning data
- State is read/written to disk -- no in-memory alternative
- The `lru_cache` on `_find_source_files_cached` persists across test runs
- No dependency injection -- functions reach into modules to get globals

The `compute_narrative` function and the scoring module (`scoring.py`) are the most testable parts -- they are mostly pure functions that take dicts and return dicts. But the detector phases and the CLI layer are tightly coupled to the filesystem and global state.

---

### 8. Configuration Management

**Severity: Low-Medium**

Several values are hardcoded that could be configurable:

**a) Thresholds** -- While `large_threshold` and `complexity_threshold` are configurable per language on `LangConfig`, many detector-internal thresholds are hardcoded:
- `MIN_SAMPLE = 200` in `scoring.py` -- dampening threshold for dimension weights
- `complexity_only_min: int = 35` in `merge_structural_signals`
- `_REMINDER_DECAY_THRESHOLD = 3` in `narrative.py`
- `loc_range: tuple[int, int] = (50, 200)` in `make_single_use_findings`
- Scan history kept to 20 entries (`state.py:375`)
- File smell entry counts limited to 10 lines in `make_smell_findings`

**b) Hardcoded paths** -- Font paths in `scorecard.py` are OS-specific:
```python
# scorecard.py:41-43
candidates = [
    "/System/Library/Fonts/SFNSMono.ttf",
    "/usr/share/fonts/truetype/dejavu/DejaVuSansMono.ttf",
```
This has a fallback to `ImageFont.load_default()` which is fine, but the font selection is not configurable.

**c) `SRC_PATH`** in `utils.py`:
```python
SRC_PATH = PROJECT_ROOT / os.environ.get("DESLOPPIFY_SRC", "src")
```
This is only used in the TypeScript coupling phase and assumes a `src/shared/` + `src/tools/` directory structure. It is effectively hardcoded to one project's layout.

**d) `get_area()` in `utils.py`** (lines 294-307) has hardcoded path prefixes (`src/tools/`, `src/shared/components/`, etc.) that are specific to a particular TypeScript project. This should be configurable per-project or per-language.

---

### 9. Python Version Compatibility

**Severity: Low**

The project declares `requires-python = ">=3.10"` in `pyproject.toml`. Usage of 3.10+ features:
- `match` statements: not used
- `Path | None` union syntax (PEP 604): used extensively (e.g., `state.py:36`, `cli.py:25`)
- `tuple[str, ...]` lowercase generic syntax (PEP 585): used throughout
- `from __future__ import annotations`: used in some modules (`plan.py`, `scoring.py`, `narrative.py`, `zones.py`, `lang/base.py`) but **not** in `cli.py`, `state.py`, or `utils.py`

The inconsistent use of `from __future__ import annotations` is a minor issue. Without it, `Path | None` requires Python 3.10+ at runtime (not just for type checking). With it, all annotations are strings and evaluated lazily. The modules that omit the future import but use `|` syntax will fail on Python 3.9 -- which is fine since `>=3.10` is declared, but the inconsistency suggests it was added reactively rather than as a project-wide standard.

---

### Summary: Top 5 Systemic Issues by Priority

1. **No tests** -- The biggest risk. The codebase has 15+ detectors, a scoring system, a narrative engine, and complex state management, all with zero automated tests. Any refactoring is flying blind.

2. **Global mutable state (`_extra_exclusions`, `PROJECT_ROOT`, `lang._zone_map`)** -- These make the code untestable, non-reentrant, and unsuitable for library use. The `_extra_exclusions` binding hazard is already documented as a known footgun.

3. **Untyped dict-based data model** -- Findings, state, potentials, detector results, and metrics are all `dict` with no schema enforcement. A `TypedDict` or dataclass layer would catch bugs at development time rather than runtime.

4. **CLI layer leakage** -- Private `cli.py` functions (`_state_path`, `_write_query`, `_resolve_lang`) are imported by every command module. `sys.exit()` is called in utility functions. The `args` namespace is used to smuggle runtime state. This couples the entire codebase to the argparse CLI entry point.

5. **Double state loading and mutation-in-place** -- State is loaded multiple times per command, mutated in place by `merge_scan` and `compute_narrative`, and written atomically at the end. There is no clear ownership of when state may be modified, and the narrative "compute" function has a hidden write side-effect.</result>

## New Meta-Detectors (6 implemented)

All detect issues that our code review found manually:

| Detector | What it catches | Found in our codebase |
|----------|----------------|----------------------|
| `subprocess_no_timeout` | `subprocess.run()` etc. without `timeout=` | scorecard.py (fixed it too) |
| `mutable_class_var` | `class Foo: data = []` shared across instances | 0 (already fixed) |

## 1. TIER_WEIGHTS Defined Twice (Inconsistency / Fragile Coupling)

**Files:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py` (line 120), `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/scoring.py` (line 34)

**Severity:** Medium

`TIER_WEIGHTS = {1: 1, 2: 2, 3: 3, 4: 4}` is defined identically in both `state.py` and `scoring.py`. Additionally, `narrative.py` (line 266) imports it from `scoring`, while `state.py` uses its own copy. The copy in `state.py` is only used by `_weighted_progress()`, which is a legacy scoring function.

**Why it matters:** If someone updates the weights in one file but not the other, the legacy score and the objective score would silently disagree. There is no indication in `state.py` that its `TIER_WEIGHTS` is a legacy duplicate.

**Suggested fix:** Either delete the `state.py` copy and import from `scoring.py`, or add a comment in `state.py` explicitly noting it is a legacy constant that must remain independent. Given that `_weighted_progress` appears to be the fallback scorer, I would import from scoring to maintain a single source of truth.

## 2. `_EXCLUDED_ZONES` in scoring.py Is a Parallel Definition

**Files:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/scoring.py` (line 46), `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/zones.py` (lines 25, 28)

**Severity:** Medium

`scoring.py` defines `_EXCLUDED_ZONES = {"test", "config", "generated", "vendor"}` as raw strings, while `zones.py` defines `EXCLUDED_ZONES` as a set of `Zone` enum values and `EXCLUDED_ZONE_VALUES` as the string equivalents. The scoring module should be using the canonical definition from zones.

**Why it matters:** If a new zone is added to `EXCLUDED_ZONES` in `zones.py` (e.g., a hypothetical `Zone.FIXTURE`), `scoring.py` would not exclude it. The `EXCLUDED_ZONE_VALUES` constant in `zones.py` exists precisely for this use case but is never imported anywhere.

**Suggested fix:** In `scoring.py`, replace `_EXCLUDED_ZONES = {"test", "config", "generated", "vendor"}` with `from .zones import EXCLUDED_ZONE_VALUES as _EXCLUDED_ZONES`.

## 3. `_analyze_debt` Computes Objective Score Without Sample Dampening

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/narrative.py` (lines 265-282)

**Severity:** Medium

`_analyze_debt` manually recomputes a tier-weighted average of dimension scores, but it does not apply the `MIN_SAMPLE` dampening factor that `compute_objective_score` in `scoring.py` applies. This means the `overall_lenient` and `overall_strict` values reported in the debt analysis will differ from the actual `objective_score` and `objective_strict` values stored in state.

```python
# narrative.py - no dampening
w = TIER_WEIGHTS.get(tier, 2)
w_sum_l += ds["score"] * w

# scoring.py - with dampening  
sample_factor = min(1.0, data.get("checks", 0) / MIN_SAMPLE)
effective_weight = w * sample_factor
```

**Why it matters:** The `overall_gap` calculation in debt analysis will produce numbers inconsistent with the actual score, potentially producing misleading gap estimates in the headline. For dimensions with small sample sizes, the gap could be significantly overstated.

**Suggested fix:** Either call `compute_objective_score` directly (once for the dimension_scores as-is, once with strict scores substituted), or replicate the dampening logic. The former is cleaner.

## 5. `compute_narrative` Mutates `state` as a Side Effect

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/narrative.py` (lines 783-786)

**Severity:** High

The docstring says "Pure functions that derive structured observations from state data. No print statements -- returns dicts." But `_compute_reminders` mutates `state["reminder_history"]` on line 786:

```python
state["reminder_history"] = reminder_history
```

This is particularly insidious because `compute_narrative` is described as pure, but it writes to the state dict that was passed in. Anyone calling `compute_narrative` for informational purposes (e.g., to preview what the narrative would look like without committing) would unknowingly increment all reminder counters.

**Why it matters:** If `compute_narrative` is called more than once per scan cycle (e.g., for display AND for query output), reminder counters get double-incremented, causing reminders to decay faster than intended. The function's contract (pure) is violated.

**Suggested fix:** Return the reminder history updates as part of the return value and let the caller decide whether to commit them to state. Or at minimum, update the docstring to clearly document the mutation.

## 6. `_compute_badge_status` Does Filesystem I/O in a "Pure" Narrative Module

In a CI environment or when PROJECT_ROOT points to a non-existent path, this silently returns false data rather than signaling an error.

## 7. `_auto_resolve_disappeared` Resolves `wontfix` Findings

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py` (lines 264-288)

**Severity:** Medium

Line 272: `if fid in current_ids or old["status"] not in ("open", "wontfix"): continue`

This means findings marked `wontfix` can be auto-resolved if they disappear from a scan. The note on line 285 acknowledges this: `"Fixed despite wontfix -- disappeared from scan (was wontfix)"`. However, this has a subtle consequence: wontfix findings are supposed to represent deliberate decisions, yet they can be silently resolved without user action. If the disappearance was due to a detector change (not an actual code fix), the wontfix decision history is lost.

**Why it matters:** A user who carefully marked findings as wontfix with explanatory notes will have their decisions silently undone if a detector is refactored or the scan scope changes. The note text says "Fixed despite wontfix" but the finding might have disappeared for a completely different reason.

**Suggested fix:** Consider keeping wontfix findings stable unless `force_resolve=True`. Or at minimum, track these differently (e.g., `status=auto_resolved_from_wontfix`) so the user can distinguish intentional cleanup from detector churn.

## 8. `save_state` Fallback Write Is Not Atomic

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py` (lines 110-116)

**Severity:** Medium

When the atomic write fails (line 110 catches `OSError`), the fallback on line 116 does `p.write_text(content)`. If the process is interrupted during this direct write, the state file will be partially written and corrupted. The backup was already made from the *previous* state, so the current scan's data could be lost.

```python
except OSError:
    # Fallback to direct write if atomic write fails
    try:
        os.unlink(tmp_path)
    except (OSError, UnboundLocalError):
        pass
    p.write_text(content)  # Non-atomic, can leave corrupt state
```

**Why it matters:** The whole point of the atomic write was to prevent corruption. The fallback undoes that guarantee silently. If `mkstemp` fails (e.g., disk full), `write_text` will also likely fail, but it could also partially succeed.

**Suggested fix:** If atomic write fails, log a warning rather than silently falling back to a non-atomic write. If you must fall back, at least ensure the backup is preserved and the user is warned.

---

## 9. `_find_source_files_cached` Uses `lru_cache` on a Function That Reads Global Mutable State

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/utils.py` (lines 217-241)

**Severity:** High

The function is decorated with `@lru_cache(maxsize=16)` and reads `_extra_exclusions` from the module-level mutable global. While `set_exclusions` does call `_find_source_files_cached.cache_clear()`, there is a subtlety: `_extra_exclusions` is baked into `all_exclusions` on line 224, but it is NOT part of the cache key. This means:

1. If `set_exclusions` is called with `["foo"]`
2. Then `_find_source_files_cached("src", (".py",))` is called and cached
3. Then `set_exclusions` is called with `["bar"]` (which clears the cache)
4. Then `_find_source_files_cached("src", (".py",))` is called again -- correctly recomputed

This actually works because `set_exclusions` clears the entire cache. BUT: if anyone ever calls `_find_source_files_cached` with `exclusions=("custom",)` first, then changes `_extra_exclusions` and calls it again with the same arguments, the global exclusion change is invisible because the cache key (`path`, `extensions`, `exclusions`) did not change and the cache was not cleared manually.

More practically: the function concatenates `exclusions + _extra_exclusions` but only `exclusions` is in the cache key. The cache can serve stale results if the function is called with explicit `exclusions` before and after a global exclusion change.

**Why it matters:** This is a correctness issue hiding behind the fact that the current code path always calls `cache_clear()`. But it is a latent bug waiting to surface if the call patterns change.

**Suggested fix:** Include `_extra_exclusions` in the cache key explicitly, or document the coupling between `set_exclusions` and this cache very prominently.

## 11. `_detect_phase` Returns "early_momentum" Unconditionally for Scans 3-5

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/narrative.py` (lines 151-157)

**Severity:** Medium

```python
if len(history) <= 5:
    if len(history) >= 2:
        first = history[0].get("objective_strict")
        last = history[-1].get("objective_strict")
        if first is not None and last is not None and last > first:
            return "early_momentum"
    return "early_momentum"  # <-- Falls through here even when score dropped
```

If `len(history)` is 2-5, and both `first` and `last` are not None, but `last <= first` (score went DOWN), the code falls through the `if` and hits the unconditional `return "early_momentum"` on line 157. This means a project whose score regressed on scan 3-5 gets labeled "early_momentum" instead of "regression".

Wait -- actually, regression is checked earlier (lines 135-139). So if the most recent scan dropped, it would return "regression" before reaching this code. The issue is when the OVERALL trajectory is flat or negative (first scan score >= last scan score) but the most recent individual scan was not a drop -- e.g., scores went 80, 78, 79. The regression check catches 80->78, but on scan 3 (79 vs 78, which went up), it passes regression AND stagnation (spread=2 > 0.5), falls to line 151, `last(79) <= first(80)`, falls through to `return "early_momentum"`.

**Why it matters:** A project that has been sliding backwards overall but had a slight uptick on the most recent scan gets the cheery "early_momentum" phase, which drives optimistic headlines and action framing. This feels dishonest.

**Suggested fix:** Only return "early_momentum" when the score is genuinely trending up, or add a neutral "early" phase for ambiguous trajectories.

## 14. `_weighted_progress` Treats `false_positive` as "Addressed" But Not as "Fixed"

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py` (lines 138-150)

**Severity:** Low

```python
if f["status"] != "open":
    addressed_w += w
if f["status"] in ("fixed", "auto_resolved", "false_positive"):
    fixed_w += w
```

`wontfix` counts as "addressed" but not "fixed." `false_positive` counts as both. This is a defensible choice, but it means marking something as `false_positive` improves both scores, while `wontfix` only improves the lenient score. The naming "strict_score" (from line 150) vs "score" is confusing because in the objective scoring system, "strict" means wontfix counts as failure. Here, "strict" means wontfix does NOT count as fixed. The two systems use "strict" to mean slightly different things.

**Why it matters:** This is a conceptual inconsistency. In the objective scoring system (scoring.py), strict means `{"open", "wontfix"}` are failures. In the legacy system (state.py), strict means only `{"fixed", "auto_resolved", "false_positive"}` are successes. These happen to produce the same arithmetic, but the framing differs, and if they ever diverge, bugs will follow.

## 15. `FileZoneMap.get` Returns `Zone.PRODUCTION` for Unknown Paths

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/zones.py` (lines 130-132)

**Severity:** Medium

```python

    return self._map.get(path, Zone.PRODUCTION)
```

The zone map is built from the file list passed at construction. If a finding references a file that was not in the original file list (e.g., because it was discovered through imports rather than file walking), `get` silently returns `PRODUCTION`. This means the file's zone is never computed, and it defaults to production scoring.

Looking at `plan.py` line 84:
```python

```

If `f.get("file", "")` is an empty string, it gets `PRODUCTION`. If it is a relative path but the zone map was built with absolute paths (or vice versa), it also gets `PRODUCTION`.

**Why it matters:** Files discovered through dep graph traversal but not through the file finder could escape zone classification entirely, potentially being scored as production when they are actually test files.

**Suggested fix:** Track which paths have been classified vs. defaulted. Consider a `get_or_classify` method that runs the rules on-the-fly for unknown paths.

## 21. `_detect_milestone` Checks Milestones in Priority Order But Returns First Match

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/narrative.py` (lines 312-348)

**Severity:** Low

If a project crosses both 80% and clears all T1/T2 in the same scan, only "Crossed 80% strict!" is returned. If in the next scan they cross 90%, they get "Crossed 90% strict!" but the T1/T2 clear milestone was already silently consumed. The milestone detection is stateless -- it re-derives from current state each time, so the T1/T2 clear milestone will fire on the NEXT scan instead. This is not necessarily wrong, but it means milestones can be silently swallowed.

The more serious edge case: lines 333-338 check `t1_open == 0 and t2_open == 0` and trigger "All T1 and T2 items cleared!" based on whether there were ever any T1/T2 items. But this fires on EVERY scan where T1/T2 are clear, not just the first time. On a project that never had T1/T2 issues, this never fires (total is 0). On a project that cleared them 5 scans ago, this fires every time, but it is suppressed by the earlier checks for score crossing.

**Why it matters:** Milestone detection is stateless, so it can either fire too often or get swallowed by priority ordering. A "seen milestones" set in state would be more robust.

This uses `in` (substring containment), not `endswith`. So a pattern like `.test` would match `file.test.ts`, `file.testing.py`, and even `.test_helpers.py`. The docstring says "basename ends-with" but the implementation does `in`.

**Suggested fix:** Change `return pattern in basename` to `return basename.endswith(pattern)` for consistency with the documented behavior and the `_` prefix handling.

## 24. `is_ignored` Pattern Logic Has Ambiguity

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py` (lines 187-199)

**Severity:** Low

```python
if "*" in pat:
    target = finding_id if "::" in pat else file
    if fnmatch.fnmatch(target, pat):
        return True
elif "::" in pat:
    if finding_id.startswith(pat):
        return True
elif file == pat or file == rel(pat):
    return True
```

The pattern `foo::*` takes the glob branch (has `*`) and matches against `finding_id` (has `::`). The pattern `foo::bar` takes the prefix branch. But `foo::bar*baz` takes the glob branch and matches against `finding_id`. This is fine, but the pattern `*/foo.py` takes the glob branch and matches against `file` (no `::`). If the user writes `*.py::*`, it matches against `finding_id` because it has `::`. This could be surprising -- a glob that looks like a file pattern but is actually matched against the finding ID.

**Why it matters:** The priority of `*` over `::` in pattern parsing means some patterns can be interpreted in non-obvious ways. This is documented nowhere.

## 25. `_find_suspect_detectors` Threshold of 3 Is Arbitrary and Undocumented

**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py` (lines 258-260)

**Severity:** Low

```python
if n >= 3:
    suspect.add(det)
```

A detector is only flagged as "suspect" (possibly didn't run) if it had 3+ open findings before and 0 now. The threshold of 3 is a magic number. For a detector that consistently finds exactly 2 issues, going to 0 would not trigger the suspect check, potentially auto-resolving legitimate findings.

**Why it matters:** The threshold creates a blind spot for detectors with small finding counts. A detector with 2 critical findings that fails to run would have both findings auto-resolved.

## Deep Code Review: TypeScript Fixers & Infrastructure

### 1. FILE WRITE SAFETY -- The Most Critical Issue

**Non-atomic writes in all fixers.** This is the single biggest problem across the entire fixer subsystem.

The state module (`/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py`, lines 91-116) uses a proper atomic write pattern: `tempfile.mkstemp` -> `os.write` -> `os.fsync` -> `os.replace`, with a `.json.bak` backup. But *none* of the fixers follow this pattern. Every fixer writes user source code with a bare `p.write_text(new_content)`:

- `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/common.py` line 137
- `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/logs.py` line 71
- `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/params.py` line 85
- `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/vars.py` line 109

If a fixer crashes mid-file (OOM, `SIGKILL`, power loss, disk full), the file is truncated or corrupted with no backup and no way to recover. This is particularly dangerous because:

- The fixers modify *the user's source code*, not an internal state file.
- A user might run fixers on an uncommitted working tree.
- The `apply_fixer` function in `common.py` processes files in a loop, so a crash partway through leaves some files modified and others not -- an inconsistent state.

**Recommendation:** Either (a) write to a temp file and `os.replace`, or (b) at minimum create a `.bak` copy before writing. Given that these are user source files, option (a) is better. Alternatively, since this tool is presumably used in git repos, you could at least warn if there are uncommitted changes.

---

### 2. TEMPLATE LITERAL / BACKTICK STRING HANDLING IN BRACKET TRACKERS

**Bug: Template literal expressions are not handled.** The `find_balanced_end` function in `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/common.py` (lines 10-61) and the similar brace tracker in `_find_if_chain_end` in `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/if_chain.py` (lines 34-71) both track strings by looking for matching quote characters. For backtick strings, they correctly enter "in string" mode when they see a backtick. However, they do NOT handle template literal expressions (`${...}`).

Consider this TypeScript:

```typescript
console.log(`value is ${obj.method({key: "val"})}`)
```

The string tracker will enter backtick-string mode at the first backtick. It will then look for a closing backtick. But inside the `${}`, there are braces and a double-quoted string. Since the tracker does not exit "in string" mode for `${`, it will see the `"` inside the template expression as the start of a new string, get confused about nesting, and potentially exit the backtick string prematurely at the `"val"` closing quote. The actual behavior is: it enters backtick string mode, then when it hits the `}` after `"val"`, it does NOT see it as a brace because `in_str` is set. But when it hits the closing `)` backtick, it exits the string correctly *by accident* in simple cases.

However, for a case like:

```typescript
console.log(`a]b`)
```

The tracker will see `]` and decrement `bracket_depth` *inside the string*, which is wrong. Actually wait -- let me re-read the code. On line 35-39, when `in_str` is truthy, the character is skipped entirely (including bracket characters). So brackets inside single/double/backtick strings ARE correctly ignored. The real problem is:

```typescript
const x = `hello ${func({a: 1})} world`;
if (x) {
```

Here the backtick opens on the first line. The tracker enters string mode. It sees `${` but ignores it because it's "in string." It stays in string mode until it hits the closing backtick. So the `{a: 1}` braces inside the template expression are correctly ignored. This actually works by coincidence for *simple* template literals.

But consider:

```typescript
const x = `hello ${"`"}` // string ends after the escaped backtick
```

The tracker will see the backtick inside `${...}` and think the template literal has ended. Then it will see the remaining `"}` and the final backtick as regular code followed by a new string. This is a genuine bug but likely very rare in practice.

**More practically**, here is a real failure case:

```typescript
const msg = `line1
line2
line3`;
console.log(msg);
```

If the scanner reports `console.log(msg)` on line 4, `find_balanced_end` starts at line 4, sees `(` and `)` on the same line, and returns line 4. That is correct. But if a multi-line template literal contains parentheses:

```typescript
console.log(`
  result: (${value})
`);
```

Starting from the `console.log` line, the tracker would see `(` twice (from the call and from the literal text), enter string mode at the backtick, then correctly ignore content until the closing backtick on line 3, then see `)` and `;`. This would work correctly. So the template literal issue is mostly theoretical for the actual usage patterns.

**Escape sequence handling is also simplified.** The `prev_ch != "\\"` check on line 36 of `common.py` handles single escapes like `\"` but NOT double escapes like `\\"` (an escaped backslash followed by a real quote). This is the classic regex-style escape bug. For:

```typescript
const s = "path\\";  // string ends at the second backslash
```

The tracker will see `\\` and set `prev_ch = "\\"`. Then it sees `"` with `prev_ch == "\\"`, so it thinks this quote is escaped and stays in string mode. But actually the backslash is itself escaped, and the quote *does* end the string. This could cause the tracker to miss the end of a statement and skip over real code.

---

### 3. BUGS AND LOGIC ERRORS

#### 3a. `_is_param_context` always returns True when paren_depth goes negative

In `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/params.py`, lines 92-117:

```python
def _is_param_context(lines: list[str], line_idx: int) -> bool:
    ...
    for back in range(0, 15):
        ...
        if paren_depth < 0:
            for check_idx in range(max(0, idx - 1), idx + 1):
                ...
                if re.search(r"(?:function\s+\w+|catch|\w+\s*=\s*(?:async\s+)?)\s*$", prev):
                    return True
                if re.search(r"(?:function|catch)\s*\($", prev):
                    return True
                if prev.endswith("("):
                    return True
            return True  # <-- line 114: ALWAYS returns True
```

When `paren_depth` goes negative (meaning we found an unmatched `(`), the function checks a couple of lines for function/catch context. But after the inner for loop, it unconditionally returns `True` at line 114, regardless of whether the checks matched. This means ANY code inside parentheses will be treated as a parameter context. For example:

```typescript
const result = someArray.filter(
  item => item.unusedVar > 0  // <-- unusedVar flagged on this line
);
```

Walking back, we find the opening `(` on the previous line. The checks for `function`, `catch`, etc. don't match `filter(`, but the function returns `True` anyway. In this specific case, `filter` is indeed a callback so the behavior is accidentally correct. But for something like:

```typescript
if (
  unusedVar === true  // <-- reported here
) {
```

This would also return `True`, treating a condition variable as a "parameter" and prefixing it with `_`. That would change semantics.

#### 3b. `_remove_empty_blocks` can remove non-empty blocks

In `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/logs.py`, lines 230-249, the multi-line empty block detection:

```python
if stripped.endswith("{"):
    j = i + 1
    while j < len(lines) and lines[j].strip() == "":
        j += 1
    if j < len(lines) and lines[j].strip() in ("}", "});"):
        if re.match(r"\s*(?:if|else\s+if)\s*\(", stripped):
            changed = True
            i = j + 1
            continue
```

This checks if a line ending with `{` is followed by only blank lines and then a `}`. But it only looks at `lines[j]` -- the first non-blank line after the opening brace. If the block contains a single blank line followed by actual code followed by a closing brace, the `while` loop skips the blank line, then `lines[j]` is the actual code line, which is not `}` or `});`, so it moves on. That seems fine.

But consider:

```typescript
if (condition) {

}
// more code
```

Here `j` skips the blank line and finds `}`. The code removes lines `i` through `j` (inclusive). This is correct for empty blocks. However, what if log removal left behind:

```typescript
if (condition) {
}
```

Where there are no blank lines? Then `j = i + 1`, `lines[j].strip()` is `}`, and it gets removed. That's also correct.

The real risk is that this runs in a `while changed` loop. After one pass removes some blocks, the next pass operates on different line indices. This should be safe since `lines` is rebuilt each iteration, but the multi-pass approach with index tracking is fragile.

#### 3c. Dead variable detection has false positives for shadowed names

In `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/logs.py`, `_find_dead_log_variables` (lines 115-152) looks for variable declarations whose name appears only in removed log lines. The check is file-wide: it scans ALL non-removed lines for the identifier. But it uses a simple `\b` word-boundary match, which means:

```typescript
const debugData = fetchDebugInfo();
console.log(debugData);

// In a completely different scope:
function other() {
  const debugData = getOtherData(); // shadows the outer one
  return debugData;
}
```

Here `debugData` appears in non-removed lines (inside `other()`), so the outer declaration is NOT removed. That is correct -- the false positive is in the *safe* direction (not removing enough). But the opposite case is dangerous:

```typescript
function foo() {
  const val = compute();
  console.log(val);
}

function bar() {
  // val is not used anywhere in bar
}
```

Here `val` is declared at one scope, used only in the log. The scanner checks ALL lines in the file for `val`. Since `val` only appears on the declaration line and the removed log line, the declaration gets removed. This is correct in this case.

But what if there is a property or string that contains the identifier?

```typescript
const debugFlag = true;
console.log(debugFlag);
const config = { debugFlag: false }; // <-- "debugFlag" appears here as a property name
```

The regex `\b debugFlag \b` will match the property name `debugFlag` on the last line, so the declaration will NOT be removed. This is a false negative (safe direction). Overall, the dead variable detection is conservative enough to avoid data loss, though it may miss some removals.

#### 3d. Exports fixer: `export { name1, name2 }` regex does not handle multi-line

In `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/exports.py`, line 50:

```python
brace_content = re.search(r"\{([^}]*)\}", stripped)
```

This looks for `{...}` on a single line. If the export statement spans multiple lines:

```typescript
export {
  name1,
  name2,
}
```

The regex will NOT match because `stripped` is only the first line (`export {`). The `[^}]*` won't match across lines. The fixer will silently skip this case. Not a data-corruption bug, but a functional gap.

#### 3e. Import fixer: `_is_import_complete` may false-positive on complex from clauses

In `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/fixers/imports.py`, lines 98-108:

```python
def _is_import_complete(text: str) -> bool:
    stripped = text.strip()
    if stripped.endswith(";"):
        return True
    if "from " in stripped and ("'" in stripped.split("from ")[-1] or '"' in stripped.split("from ")[-1]):
        after_from = stripped.split("from ", 1)[-1].strip()
        if (after_from.startswith("'") and after_from.count("'") >= 2) or \
           (after_from.startswith('"') and after_from.count('"') >= 2):
            return True
    return False
```

This splits on `"from "` to find the module specifier. But consider:

```typescript
import { transformFrom } from "./transform";
```

`stripped.split("from ")` produces `["import { trans", "} ", "./transform\";"]`. Then `split("from ", 1)[-1]` gives `"} from \"./transform\";"`. This actually handles it correctly because of `split("from ", 1)`.

But what about:

```typescript
import {
  something  // no semicolon, no from yet
```

`"from "` is not in the text, so it returns `False` and the loop continues collecting lines. Correct.

However, if someone writes a comment with "from" in it:

```typescript
import { // imported from legacy module
  oldFunc
} from "./legacy";
```

On the first line alone: `stripped = "import { // imported from legacy module"`. `"from "` IS in the text. `split("from ", 1)[-1]` is `"legacy module"`. `after_from.startswith("'")` is False, `after_from.startswith('"')` is False. So `_is_import_complete` returns False, and the loop continues. This is actually correct.

---

### 4. CONSISTENCY ISSUES

#### 4a. Three different file-processing patterns

The fixers use three different patterns for processing files:

1. **`apply_fixer` template** (in `common.py`): Used by `exports.py`, `if_chain.py`, `useeffect.py`, and `imports.py` (after pre-filtering). This is the clean pattern.

2. **Manual file loop with `apply_fixer`-like structure** (copy-pasted): Used by `logs.py`, `params.py`, and `vars.py`. These replicate the `by_file` grouping, `Path` resolution, `read_text`/`write_text`, and error handling manually.

The manual file loops in `logs.py`, `params.py`, and `vars.py` are essentially copy-pasted versions of `apply_fixer` with slight variations. This makes maintenance harder and introduces subtle inconsistencies. For example, `logs.py` returns `tags` and `log_count` instead of `removed`, and `vars.py` returns a tuple `(results, skip_reasons)` -- both of which are legitimate reasons to not use `apply_fixer`. But `params.py` has no such reason and could use `apply_fixer`.

#### 4b. Return type inconsistency

- `fix_unused_vars` returns `tuple[list[dict], dict[str, int]]`
- All other fixers return `list[dict]`
- The `dict` in the return varies: some have `removed` (list of names), `logs.py` has `tags` and `log_count`, `exports.py` overrides `lines_removed` to 0 after the fact

This inconsistency means the caller has to know which fixer it is calling to handle the return value correctly.

---

### 5. ENCODING ISSUES

No fixer specifies an encoding for `read_text()` or `write_text()`. These default to `locale.getpreferredencoding()`, which is usually UTF-8 on modern systems but could be something else. More importantly:

- `read_text()` will raise `UnicodeDecodeError` on binary files or files with invalid encoding, which IS caught.
- But `write_text()` could potentially re-encode content differently than it was read if the system encoding is not UTF-8. For example, if a file contains Windows-1252 characters and the system encoding is UTF-8, `read_text()` might succeed (if the bytes happen to be valid UTF-8) but the content round-trips correctly. If not, the `UnicodeDecodeError` is caught and the file is skipped.

The `state.py` module uses `content.encode()` explicitly, which defaults to UTF-8. The fixers should ideally read with `encoding="utf-8"` explicitly, or better yet, read as bytes and only decode/re-encode the parts they need to modify.

---

### 6. SCORECARD MODULE

`/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/scorecard.py` is well-structured. A few observations:

#### 6a. `_score_color` tuple return type

Line 30:

```python
return tuple(int(b * 0.55 + g * 0.45) for b, g in zip(base, gray))
```

This returns a `tuple` (not `tuple[int, int, int]`), which means the type checker would infer `tuple[int, ...]`. The `fill` parameter of PIL's `draw.text` expects a color tuple, so this works at runtime but is technically type-imprecise.

#### 6b. Subprocess calls in `_get_project_name` are defensive

The `gh` CLI call has a 5-second timeout and catches `FileNotFoundError`, `CalledProcessError`, and `TimeoutExpired`. The git fallback also handles errors. The final fallback is `PROJECT_ROOT.name`. This is solid.

#### 6c. Font loading is macOS-biased

The font candidates in `_load_font` list macOS paths first, then Linux paths. There are no Windows font paths. `ImageFont.load_default()` as a fallback should work everywhere but will produce less polished output on Windows. Not a bug, but worth noting.

#### 6d. No error handling if Pillow is not installed

`generate_scorecard` does `from PIL import Image, ImageDraw` inside the function body, with a `# noqa: deferred -- Pillow is optional` comment. But there is no try/except around it. If Pillow is not installed, this will raise `ModuleNotFoundError` which will propagate up to the caller. The caller presumably handles this, but the function itself does not.

---

### 7. VISUALIZE MODULE

`/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/visualize.py` has a few issues:

#### 7a. XSS risk in HTML template

Line 121-127:

```python
html = html.replace("__TREE_DATA__", tree_json)
html = html.replace("__TOTAL_FILES__", str(total_files))
...
```

`tree_json` is produced by `json.dumps(tree)`, which escapes strings correctly for JSON. However, the JSON is embedded directly into a `<script>` tag. If a filename contains `</script>`, `json.dumps` will NOT escape it (since `</script>` is valid JSON string content), and this would break out of the script tag. This is a classic XSS vector in server-rendered HTML, though in this case the output is a local file, so the practical risk is low. Still, using `json.dumps(tree, ensure_ascii=True)` or escaping `</` as `<\/` would be safer.

#### 7b. Hardcoded "src" root assumption

In `_build_tree` (line 46-51):

```python
root: dict = {"name": "src", "children": {}}
for f in files:
    parts = f["path"].split("/")
    if parts and parts[0] == "src":
        parts = parts[1:]
```

And in `generate_tree_text` (line 265):

```python
if parts and parts[0] == "src":
    parts = parts[1:]
```

This assumes the project's source code lives under a `src/` directory. If the scanned path is something else (like `lib/` or the project root), the tree root will still be named "src" but the path stripping won't apply. The root name should be derived from the actual scanned path.

#### 7c. Variable shadowing with `c` 

Line 83 in `_build_tree`:

```python
node["children"] = [c for c in node["children"]
                    if "loc" in c or ("children" in c and c["children"])]
```

The variable `c` in the list comprehension shadows the imported `c` (color utility) from `from .utils import PROJECT_ROOT, c, rel` at line 17. This is not a bug (the import is not used inside `_build_tree`), but it is a code smell.

#### 7d. Non-atomic HTML write

Line 131: `output.write_text(html)` -- same non-atomic write concern, though for an output artifact (not source code) this is much less concerning.

---

### 8. SEMANTIC SAFETY OF FIXERS

This is the most important subjective assessment: could these fixers break working code?

#### Exports fixer (`exports.py`): LOW RISK
Removes the `export` keyword from declarations with zero external importers. This is safe *if* the detector is correct. If the detector misses a dynamic import (`import()`) or a re-export via barrel files, removing `export` will break the build. The transformation itself is clean -- it only removes the keyword, preserving the declaration.

#### Import fixer (`imports.py`): MEDIUM RISK
Removes unused imports. TypeScript imports can have side effects:

```typescript
import "./polyfill"; // side-effect import -- no symbols used
import "reflect-metadata"; // side-effect import
```

If the detector flags these as unused, removing them would break runtime behavior. The fixer checks for `(entire import)` as a special marker, which suggests the detector handles this. But it is worth verifying.

Also, removing a `type` import that is used in a re-export could break downstream consumers even if the type is not used locally.

#### Vars fixer (`vars.py`): MEDIUM RISK
The removal of unused destructuring members is generally safe, but:

```typescript
const { unused, ...rest } = obj;
```

If `unused` is removed, `rest` would include `unused`. The fixer correctly checks for rest elements (`_REST_ELEMENT_RE.search(destr_text)`) and skips those cases. Good.

However, line 82-88:

```python
elif re.match(r"\s*(?:const|let|var)\s+\w+\s*=", stripped):
    rhs = stripped.split("=", 1)[1] if "=" in stripped else ""
    if stripped.rstrip().endswith(";") and "(" not in rhs:
        lines_to_remove.add(line_idx)
```

This removes standalone variable declarations where the RHS has no function calls (no `(`). But what about:

```typescript
const unusedTimer = Date.now;  // no parens, but assigning a function reference
```

Or property access with side effects (getters):

```typescript
const unused = obj.computedProperty;  // getter might have side effects
```

The `"(" not in rhs` heuristic is too simplistic. It correctly avoids removing `const x = foo()` but misses side-effect-ful expressions without parentheses.

#### Params fixer (`params.py`): LOW RISK
Prefixing unused params with `_` is semantically neutral in most cases. The param is still there, it just gets a new name. The risk is if the parameter name is used in string interpolation or reflection. In TypeScript, this would be unusual.

However, the `re.sub(r"\b" + re.escape(name) + r"\b", new_name, src, count=1)` on line 72 will rename the FIRST occurrence of the name on the line. If the parameter name appears in a default value before the actual parameter:

```typescript
function foo(name = defaultName, name2: string) // if "name" is unused
```

Wait, this would rename the `name` parameter itself (first occurrence), which is correct. But what about:

```typescript
function process(data, metadata = data.length) // "data" reported unused
```

The `count=1` would rename only the first `data` to `_data`, leaving `metadata = data.length` unchanged. Now `data.length` references a variable that does not exist (it was renamed to `_data`). This is a **real bug that could break code**. The correct fix would be to rename ALL occurrences of the parameter on its declaration line, or to use column-precise replacement (which the fixer does try first if `col` is available).

#### Logs fixer (`logs.py`): MEDIUM-HIGH RISK
This is the most aggressive fixer. It removes console.log statements AND:
- Orphaned comments above them
- Variables only used in removed logs
- Empty blocks left behind

The cascading removal is where risk accumulates. Removing a log -> removing a variable -> removing an empty block -> collapsing blank lines. Each step compounds the chance of error. The `_remove_empty_blocks` function is particularly aggressive, running in a loop until stable.

The `_remove_empty_blocks` function (line 183-271) handles `if/else`, `useEffect`, `.then/.catch`, and multi-line empty callbacks. For multi-line empty callbacks:

```python
if re.search(r"\(\s*(?:\([^)]*\))?\s*=>\s*\{\s*$", stripped):
    closing = lines[j].strip()
    if closing in ("});", "},"):
        changed = True
        i = j + 1
        continue
```

This removes a callback like `(() => {\n})` but only if the closing is `});` or `},`. If the callback has a return type annotation or other syntax after the closing brace, it is skipped. This is conservative enough.

#### useEffect fixer (`useeffect.py`): LOW RISK
Simple deletion of entire `useEffect` calls that are empty. Uses `find_balanced_end` to find the extent. If `find_balanced_end` returns `None` (cannot find balanced end), the entry is skipped. Safe.

#### If-chain fixer (`if_chain.py`): LOW RISK
Deletes if/else chains where all branches are empty. The `_find_if_chain_end` function tracks braces and looks for `else` continuations. If it cannot find the end (exceeds 100 lines), it returns `start`, which means only the start line would be removed. Actually, looking more carefully at line 71: `return start` means the range `(start, start + 1)` is a single line removal. If the if chain is actually multi-line but the tracker failed, this would leave a partial deletion (just the `if` line removed but the braces and else remaining). This could produce invalid syntax.

---

### 9. SUMMARY OF FINDINGS

**Critical:**
1. **Non-atomic file writes for user source code** -- all fixers use bare `write_text()`. A crash mid-write could corrupt source files with no backup.
2. **Params fixer `count=1` substitution bug** -- renaming only the first occurrence of a parameter name can break references to that parameter in default values on the same line.

**High:**

     ```python
     def _extract_ts_params(sig: str) -> list[str]:
         """Extract parameter names from a TS function signature string."""
         depth = 0
         start = -1
         end = -1
         for idx, ch in enumerate(sig):
             if ch == '(':
                 if depth == 0:
                     start = idx
                 depth += 1
             elif ch == ')':
                 depth -= 1
                 if depth == 0:
                     end = idx
                     break
         if start < 0 or end < 0:
             m = re.search(r'=\s*(\w+)\s*=>', sig)
             if m:
                 return [m.group(1)]
             return []
         param_str = sig[start + 1:end]
         if not param_str.strip():
             return []
         inner = param_str.strip()
         if inner.startswith('{'):
             brace_end = inner.find('}')
             if brace_end > 0:
                 inner_params = inner[1:brace_end]
                 return _parse_param_names(inner_params)
         return _parse_param_names(param_str)
     ```
     - Replaced naive brace counting with string-aware version (skips quotes and // comments)
     - Added params extraction from signature lines before first `{`

     ```python
     def _safe_write(filepath: str | Path, content: str) -> None:
         """Atomic write: write to temp file then rename."""
         p = Path(filepath)
         tmp = p.with_suffix(p.suffix + ".tmp")
         try:
             tmp.write_text(content)
             os.replace(str(tmp), str(p))
         except BaseException:
             tmp.unlink(missing_ok=True)
             raise
     ```
     - Both single-file and directory move now have try/except with rollback

     ```python
     def _strip_ts_comments(text: str) -> str:
         """Strip // and /* */ comments while preserving strings."""
         result: list[str] = []
         i = 0
         in_str = None
         while i < len(text):
             ch = text[i]
             if in_str:
                 if ch == '\\' and i + 1 < len(text):
                     result.append(text[i:i + 2])
                     i += 2
                     continue
                 if ch == in_str:
                     in_str = None
                 result.append(ch)
                 i += 1
             elif ch in ('"', "'", '`'):
                 in_str = ch
                 result.append(ch)
                 i += 1
             elif ch == '/' and i + 1 < len(text):
                 if text[i + 1] == '/':
                     nl = text.find('\n', i)
                     if nl == -1: break
                     i = nl
                 elif text[i + 1] == '*':
                     end = text.find('*/', i + 2)
                     if end == -1: break
                     i = end + 2
                 else:
                     result.append(ch)
                     i += 1
             else:
                 result.append(ch)
                 i += 1
         return ''.join(result)
     ```
     - Replaced 3 instances of naive `re.sub(r"//[^\n]*", "")` with `_strip_ts_comments()`

All 17 tasks are now complete. Here's the summary of the final 3 meta-detectors:

---

**Implemented 4 new Python smell detectors + 1 bug fix:**

1. **`unreachable_code`** (severity: high) — AST-based detector that walks statement blocks and flags any code after an unconditional `return`, `raise`, `break`, or `continue`. Recurses into compound statements (if/else/try/except bodies).

2. **`constant_return`** (severity: medium) — AST-based detector that finds functions with multiple return sites where every return yields the same constant value (True, False, a number, etc.). Excludes `None` returns (normal procedure pattern) and trivial stubs (already caught by `dead_function`).

3. **`regex_backtrack`** (severity: high) — AST-based detector that analyzes regex patterns in `re.compile()`, `re.search()`, etc. for nested quantifiers that could cause catastrophic backtracking (ReDoS). Correctly filters out safe patterns: `?` as outer quantifier, negated character classes `[^X]*`, and literal-anchored inner groups like `\.\w+`.

4. **`naive_comment_strip`** (severity: medium) — AST-based detector that finds `re.sub()` calls stripping comment syntax (`//`, `/*`, `#`) without string awareness, which corrupts URLs and other legitimate uses of these characters inside string literals.

5. **Bug fix**: `react.py` still had the naive `re.sub(r"//[^\n]*", "")` comment stripping — replaced with string-aware `_strip_ts_comments()` from `_smell_helpers.py`.

**Test results**: All detectors validated with synthetic test cases (correct true positives, correct true negative filtering). Both repos scan cleanly — Python 98.3/100, TypeScript 87.5/100.

# Error Handling Audit Report: `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/`

## CRITICAL FINDINGS

### 1. TS `subprocess.run` with no timeout (can hang forever)
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/detectors/unused.py`, line 30-33
```python
result = subprocess.run(
    ["npx", "tsc", "--project", str(tmp_path), "--noEmit"],
    capture_output=True, text=True, cwd=PROJECT_ROOT,
)
```
**Issue:** No `timeout` parameter. The `npx tsc` subprocess has no time limit.
**Scenario:** On a massive TypeScript codebase with complex type resolution, or if `npx` stalls waiting for input (e.g., prompting to install a package), this call will block indefinitely, making the entire CLI unresponsive. Since this is wrapped in a `try/finally` to clean up the temp tsconfig, the tool just freezes.
**Severity:** CRITICAL. The Python `ruff` and `pyflakes` equivalents correctly use `timeout=60`.

### 2. `move` command applies changes non-atomically -- partial failure corrupts code
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/commands/move.py`, lines 136-157 (single-file) and 369-393 (directory)
```python
def _apply_changes(source_abs, dest_abs, importer_changes, self_changes):
    shutil.move(source_abs, dest_abs)
    if self_changes:
        content = Path(dest_abs).read_text()
        for old_str, new_str in self_changes:
            content = content.replace(old_str, new_str)
        Path(dest_abs).write_text(content)
    for filepath, replacements in importer_changes.items():
        content = Path(filepath).read_text()
        for old_str, new_str in replacements:
            content = content.replace(old_str, new_str)
        Path(filepath).write_text(content)
```
**Issue:** No try/except at all. The file is moved first, then import references are updated in a loop. If any `read_text()` or `write_text()` fails (permission denied, disk full, locked file), the file has been moved but only some importers have been updated. The codebase is left in a broken state with inconsistent imports.
**Scenario:** Moving a file that is imported by 20 other files. File #8 is read-only. Files 1-7 are updated, the rest are not, and the user has no record of what was partially done. The code no longer compiles.
**Severity:** CRITICAL. This is a destructive operation with no rollback mechanism and no error handling.

### 3. `_fix_logs` writes files with no error handling
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/detectors/logs.py`, lines 88-101
```python
def _fix_logs(by_file):
    removed = 0
    for filepath, file_entries in by_file.items():
        lines_to_remove = {e["line"] for e in file_entries}
        p = Path(filepath) if Path(filepath).is_absolute() else PROJECT_ROOT / filepath
        original = p.read_text()
        ...
        p.write_text("".join(new_lines))
```
**Issue:** No try/except around file I/O. An `OSError` or `UnicodeDecodeError` mid-loop will partially modify some files and crash without completing. This is the old/direct `--fix` path from `detect logs --fix`.
**Scenario:** Running `detect logs --fix` on 50 files. File #25 is on a mounted volume that disconnects. Files 1-24 are modified, 25-50 are not. No indication of what was changed vs what was not.
**Severity:** HIGH.

### 4. `_write_query` has no error handling
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/cli.py`, line 14-22
```python
def _write_query(data: dict):
    QUERY_FILE.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    QUERY_FILE.write_text(json.dumps(data, indent=2, default=str) + "\n")
    print(f"  -> query.json updated", file=sys.stderr)
```
**Issue:** No try/except. If `json.dumps` fails (non-serializable data that `default=str` cannot handle) or if the write fails (disk full, permissions), the entire command crashes after doing all its real work (scan, fix, etc.) but right before reporting success.
**Scenario:** A detector produces a finding with a non-serializable object that `default=str` chokes on (e.g., a circular reference). The entire scan completes, state is saved, but the command crashes at the very end with an unhandled exception. The user gets no output summary.
**Severity:** HIGH. This is called at the tail end of every command. A crash here loses the user-facing output while the side effects have already occurred.

---

## HIGH SEVERITY FINDINGS

### 5. `state.py:load_state` missing `OSError` catch
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py`, line 45-46
```python
try:
    data = json.loads(p.read_text())
except (json.JSONDecodeError, UnicodeDecodeError) as e:
```
**Issue:** Catches `JSONDecodeError` and `UnicodeDecodeError` but not `OSError`. If the state file exists but cannot be read (permissions, concurrent lock on Windows, corrupted filesystem), `p.read_text()` raises `OSError` which is uncaught.
**Scenario:** State file exists but with restrictive permissions (`chmod 000`). The tool crashes with an unhandled `PermissionError` (a subclass of `OSError`).
**Severity:** HIGH. The backup path and "start fresh" recovery only trigger for JSON/Unicode errors.

### 6. `state.py:save_state` fallback write has no error handling
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py`, line 116
```python
except OSError:
    # Fallback to direct write if atomic write fails
    try:
        os.unlink(tmp_path)
    except (OSError, UnboundLocalError):
        pass
    p.write_text(content)  # <-- no try/except
```
**Issue:** The fallback `p.write_text(content)` on line 116 has no error handling. If the atomic write failed due to a filesystem issue and the fallback also fails, the exception propagates up, potentially after the temp file has already been partially cleaned up.
**Scenario:** Disk full -- both atomic and direct write fail. The tool crashes with an unhandled `OSError` during state save, after a successful scan. The state file may or may not exist; the backup is the only copy.
**Severity:** HIGH.

### 7. `compute_tool_hash` has no error handling
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/utils.py`, lines 269-279
```python
def compute_tool_hash() -> str:
    h = hashlib.sha256()
    for py_file in sorted(TOOL_DIR.rglob("*.py")):
        h.update(str(py_file.relative_to(TOOL_DIR)).encode())
        h.update(py_file.read_bytes())
    return h.hexdigest()[:12]
```
**Issue:** `py_file.read_bytes()` on line 278 has no try/except. If any `.py` file in the package directory is unreadable, the entire tool hash computation crashes.
**Scenario:** A `.pyc` compilation race condition or a dangling symlink in the package directory. The scan crashes before even starting.
**Severity:** HIGH. This runs on every `scan` command (called from `merge_scan`).

### 8. Output file writes with no error handling
Multiple locations write user-specified output files with no try/except:

- `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/commands/show.py`, line 107: `Path(output_file).write_text(json.dumps(payload, indent=2) + "\n")`
- `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/commands/next.py`, line 53: `Path(output_file).write_text(json.dumps(output, indent=2) + "\n")`
- `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/commands/plan_cmd.py`, line 26: `Path(output).write_text(plan_md)`
- `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/visualize.py`, line 131: `output.write_text(html)`

**Issue:** User-provided `--output` paths are written with no error handling. `mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)` is called for the parent, but the write itself can fail.
**Scenario:** User runs `desloppify show gods --output /readonly/dir/out.json`. The `mkdir` succeeds (directory exists), but `write_text` fails with `PermissionError`. Crash.
**Severity:** MEDIUM-HIGH. User-facing output paths are the most likely to be invalid or restricted.

## MEDIUM SEVERITY FINDINGS

### 9. `scorecard.py:generate_scorecard` PIL ImportError handling is insufficient
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/commands/scan.py`, lines 264-289
```python
try:
    from ..scorecard import generate_scorecard, get_badge_config
    badge_path, disabled = get_badge_config(args)
    if not disabled and badge_path:
        generate_scorecard(state, badge_path)
        ...
except ImportError:
    pass  # Pillow not installed — skip silently
```
**Issue:** Catches `ImportError` but not `OSError` from the image save, nor other PIL-specific errors like `PIL.ImageFont.FreeTypeFont` errors if the font rendering fails. The `generate_scorecard` function does `img.save(str(output_path), "PNG", optimize=True)` with no error handling.
**Scenario:** Pillow is installed but the output path is invalid (e.g., `--badge-path /dev/null/scorecard.png`). An `OSError` propagates through, crashing the scan after all work is done.
**Severity:** MEDIUM. The badge is optional, but a crash here ruins the entire scan output.

### 10. `scorecard.py:_get_project_name` git fallback edge case
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/scorecard.py`, lines 126-140
```python
try:
    url = subprocess.check_output(
        ["git", "config", "--get", "remote.origin.url"],
        cwd=str(PROJECT_ROOT), stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL, text=True,
    ).strip()
    if url.startswith("git@") and ":" in url:
        path = url.split(":")[-1]
    else:
        path = "/".join(url.split("/")[-2:])
    return path.removesuffix(".git")
except (subprocess.CalledProcessError, FileNotFoundError, IndexError):
    return PROJECT_ROOT.name
```
**Issue:** Missing `timeout` on `subprocess.check_output`. If `git` hangs (e.g., waiting for SSH authentication), this blocks indefinitely.
**Severity:** MEDIUM. Same project name retrieval function also has a `gh` call with `timeout=5`, but the `git` fallback does not.

### 11. `_collect_codebase_metrics` silently swallows read errors
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/commands/scan.py`, lines 50-67
```python
for f in files:
    try:
        total_loc += len(Path(f).read_text().splitlines())
        dirs.add(str(Path(f).parent))
    except (OSError, UnicodeDecodeError):
        pass
```
**Issue:** `read_text()` without `errors="replace"`. Binary files or files with encoding issues raise `UnicodeDecodeError`, which is caught but means those files are silently excluded from the LOC count. Using `errors="replace"` (like `_read_file_text` does) would give a count for all files.
**Scenario:** A `.py` file with ISO-8859-1 encoding (common in older Python codebases) fails to decode with UTF-8 and is silently excluded from metrics. The LOC count is understated.
**Severity:** MEDIUM.

### 12. `_categorize_unused` returns default on exception
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/lang/typescript/detectors/unused.py`, lines 67-91
```python
def _categorize_unused(filepath: str, lineno: int) -> str:
    try:
        ...
    except (OSError, UnicodeDecodeError):
        pass
    return "vars"
```
**Issue:** On any file read failure, the function defaults to categorizing everything as "vars" instead of "imports". This silently miscategorizes findings.
**Scenario:** A file with non-UTF-8 encoding has unused imports. They all get categorized as "vars", which means `fix unused-imports` will miss them, and `fix unused-vars` will try to fix them inappropriately.
**Severity:** MEDIUM.

### 13. `_audit_excluded_dirs` does full file reads with naive search
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/commands/scan.py`, lines 30-38
```python
for filepath in scanned_files:
    try:
        abs_path = filepath if Path(filepath).is_absolute() else str(project_root / filepath)
        content = Path(abs_path).read_text(errors="replace")
        if ex_dir in content:
            ref_count += 1
            break
    except OSError:
        continue
```
**Issue:** For each excluded directory, this reads every scanned file's entire content to do a substring search. On a large codebase with many exclusions, this is O(exclusions * files * file_size).
**Scenario:** 5 `--exclude` patterns, 2000 scanned files. This reads up to 10,000 files during the audit phase (though the `break` on first match helps). Not an error handling issue per se, but the lack of a timeout or file-size limit means a single multi-GB file could cause an OOM crash.
**Severity:** MEDIUM (performance, not correctness).

### 16. `main()` only catches `KeyboardInterrupt`

**Issue:** No catch for unexpected exceptions. A `TypeError`, `KeyError`, or `AttributeError` from a bug in a detector will produce a raw Python traceback.
**Scenario:** A new detector has a bug that causes a `KeyError` on an unusual file. The user sees a multi-line traceback instead of a clean error message.
**Severity:** LOW. Raw tracebacks are ugly but technically informative for debugging. Adding a generic catch risks hiding bugs during development.

### 17. `_ResultsWithMeta` class-level mutable default

### 18. `state.py:load_state` TOCTOU on `p.exists()`
**File:** `/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py`, line 42
```python

```
**Issue:** Race condition between checking existence and reading the file on line 46. Another process could delete the file between the check and the read.
**Scenario:** Two `desloppify` processes running concurrently (e.g., in CI). One is writing state, the other is reading. The reader checks existence, the writer does `os.replace` (which is atomic but creates a brief moment of deletion on some filesystems). The reader then fails to read.
**Severity:** LOW. The CLI is typically single-process. If this race occurs, the `OSError` from `read_text()` is currently unhandled (see finding #5), which makes this an amplifier for that bug.

## KEY PATTERNS OBSERVED

**What the codebase does well:**
- `state.py:save_state` uses atomic writes with temp file + `os.replace`, plus backup copies. This is best practice.
- `_read_file_text` in `utils.py` uses `errors="replace"` to handle encoding issues gracefully.
- Detector loops generally wrap individual file processing in try/except so one bad file does not abort the entire scan.
- The fixer framework in `fixers/common.py:apply_fixer` has proper per-file error handling with user-visible warnings.

**Systemic weaknesses:**
1. **Write path vs read path asymmetry**: File reads are generally well-protected. File writes are often unprotected, especially in user-facing commands (`move`, `show --output`, `plan --output`, `detect --fix`).
2. **Subprocess timeout inconsistency**: Python subprocess calls have timeouts; the main TypeScript `tsc` call does not.
3. **Multi-step operations lack rollback**: The `move` command and `_fix_logs` modify files in a loop with no way to undo partial changes on failure.
4. **Error reporting inconsistency**: Detector errors go to `stderr` via `print(c(...), file=sys.stderr)`. State corruption warnings go to `stderr`. But command output goes to `stdout`. The `_write_query` function prints its status to `stderr`. Overall this is reasonable, but exceptions that escape error handling produce raw tracebacks to `stderr`, which is inconsistent with the clean formatting elsewhere.</result>
